5.5.
Limitations

There are a few P4 features that our
\Pi
4 prototype does not support,
mostly because they pose challenges to SMT-based approaches to
verification: hash functions, externs (a kind of foreign function
interface into the hardware), and registers. The unpredictability of
hash functions is difficult to verify, but we can either
over-approximate them as uninterpreted functions, or use a more
fine-grained approach such as concolic verification. Externs either
need to be annotated with specific types, or over-approximated as
uninterpreted functions. Registers are on-switch state that can be
modified by the packet or the controller and persists between packets.
This is tricky to represent in the semantics and has some distributed
computing concerns. We could over-approximate the behavior by
havoc-ing the values every time the register is read.

6.
Modularity Reasoning with
\Pi
4

An emerging design pattern for data plane switches is partial
programmability, e.g., Cisco’s daPIPE
(
Baldi 2019
)
, which is
designed for the Nexus 3400 switch
(
Cisco 2018
)
.
The idea is that a device vendor provides a partially-implemented pipeline
together with a set of program points where customers can inject custom code
as shown in
Figure

17
. The designer requires that customer
programs satisfy certain properties, but in current architectures, they are not automatically checked.

To illustrate, consider a deployment of the customizable pipeline in a
campus network
where network engineers want to experiment with in-band network
telemetry (INT) without perturbing the VLAN tag, which is used to
enforce security policies. Let’s say there are four classes of
traffic,
Visitor
,
Student
,
Faculty
, and

Staff
, each with unique VLAN identifiers. We want to ensure
that no matter how
Ingress
is instantiated in the left of

Figure

18
, it cannot cause students and visitors to
acquire privileges of faculty or staff—e.g., this might leak
confidential data.
With
\Pi
4, we can design a modular system that statically checks invariants on customer
programs.
Practically, we can
ensure that VLAN is not changed by checking that customer’s code has a type
like:

(x:\tau)\to\{y:\tau^{\prime}\mid x.\texttt{vlan}.\texttt{vid}=y.\texttt{vlan}.\texttt{vid}\}
,
where the
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
are appropriate for the specific
pipeline. We check, once-and-for-all, that the surrounding switch code
composes with this type, and incrementally check that the customer
code has this type (for an appropriate
\tau
).

Figure 17
.
Modular router design

6.1.
Specifying an Invariant

⬇

(

);

or

*/

3

as

(

:

)

{

.
vlan
};

4

(
vlan
)

Figure 18
.
Instantiation of modular router design; the parser (
Pre
) and deparser (
Post
) are provided by the vendor, the
Ingress
code is provided by the customer.

To further illustrate, consider the toy example shown the right of

Figure

18
, which has a VLAN instance (32 bits)
and the standard metadata used in the P4 switch model (325 bits),
including a 9-bit egress specification
stdmeta.egress_spec
, and a
12-bit vlan tag field,
vlan.vid
. The control flow simply
extracts the VLAN instance, executes the modular
Ingress

control, and then emits the VLAN header. We want the program to
typecheck with type
(x:\{x:\texttt{stdmeta}\mid|x.{pkt_{in}}|>32\})\to\Sigma y\!:{\texttt{stdmeta}}{\texttt{\char 126\relax}}.{\texttt{vlan}}{\texttt{\char 126\relax}}
.

In this example, the type ascription provides compositional reasoning—i.e.,
we don’t need to re-check that the whole pipeline is well-typed. Instead,
we check once-and-for all that
\mathit{extract(\mathtt{vlan})}
has type

(x:\{x:\texttt{stdmeta}\mid|x.{pkt_{in}}|>32\})\to(x:(\Sigma x:\texttt{stdmeta}.\texttt{ipv4}))
,
and that
\mathit{remit(\mathtt{vlan})}
has type

(x:(\{x:\Sigma x:\texttt{stdmeta}.\texttt{ipv4}\mid x.\texttt{vlan}=y.\texttt{vlan}\}))\to\Sigma y\!:{\texttt{stdmeta}}{\texttt{\char 126\relax}}.{\texttt{vlan}}{\texttt{\char 126\relax}}

in context
(y:\Sigma x\!:\texttt{stdmeta}.\texttt{ipv4})
. Both are easy to check.

Now, when we swap in different implementations for
Ingress
,
we only need to check that it has its ascribed type on
Line
3
of
Figure

18
, without
rechecking the surrounding code. With the infrastructure
\Pi
4’s type
system provides, network engineers can make changes to their
experimental module
Ingress
and check its compatibility with
the switch without re-checking the feasibility of the whole switch in a
modular fashion.

Checking Customer Programs

We now consider a collection of customer programs as shown in

Figure

that an engineer may want to install into
the switch and how
\Pi
4 prevents security vulnerabilities by ensuring
the customer code has the type annotated on Line
3
of

Figure

18
.

Figure 19
.
A collection of safe and unsafe customer implementations for the
Ingress
module from Figure
18
. Top Left:
Default
; Top Right:
Overwrite
; Bottom Left:
Table
; Bottom Right:
Unsafe Actions

Default.

Consider the empty program, shown in the top left of

Figure

18
, which would surely be the default behavior when
the programmer hasn’t written any code yet. To typecheck this no-op module, we
check that
\mathit{skip}
has the ascribed type, which it clearly does, since it does
not change the value of the program.

Overwrite.

Conversely, if the customer were to install a blatantly
incorrect program as the one in the top right of

Figure

18
, which always overwrites the VLAN tag
with the identifier reserved for faculty members, the type system
complains that the following subtyping check fails—when
x.\texttt{vlan}.\texttt{vid}
is, say,
Student
,
the two types denote disjoint sets of heaps.

\begin{array}[]{l}(x:\Sigma y\!:\texttt{stdmeta}.\texttt{vlan})\\
\quad\vdash\{w:\Sigma y\!:{\texttt{stdmeta}}{\texttt{\char 126\relax}}.{\texttt{vlan}}{\texttt{\char 126\relax}}\mid w.\texttt{vlan}.\texttt{vid}=\texttt{Faculty}\}\\
\qquad<:~\{w:\Sigma y\!:{\texttt{stdmeta}}{\texttt{\char 126\relax}}.{\texttt{vlan}}{\texttt{\char 126\relax}}\mid w.\texttt{vlan}.\texttt{vid}=x.\texttt{vlan}.\texttt{vid}\},\end{array}

Table.

We model match-action tables using an encoding similar to the
one used in
p4v

(
Liu
et al. 2018
)
, where we create an extra
header that captures the keys and selected action. Consider the

vlan
table on the bottom left of

Figure

18
, which matches on

\texttt{vlan}.\texttt{vid}
and selects one of two actions:
the first sets the
egress_spec
to
0x1FF
;
the second sets it to
0x001
. To encode this table, we create a new
header
_vlan_table
with a
12
-bit field

vid_key
and a
1
-bit field
act
, modelling
the table application via the code shown in the bottom left of
Figure

19
.
This will typecheck since no branch of the code modifies the

\texttt{vlan}.\texttt{vid}
field, and
_vlan_table
is
permitted to be valid.

Unsafe Actions.

Now consider a
vlan
table where each action

does
modify the VLAN id. For example, the table shown in the bottom right
of
Figure

18
can either set the VID to one of

\{\mathtt{Faculty},\mathtt{Staff}\}
, or, if the packet misses in the table,
to
Visitor
. Here, the VLAN id is clobbered whenever this table is applied,
triggering a violation of the subset check just as with

Overwrite
.

7.
Related Work

Formal Reasoning for P4 Programs

A number of verification approaches have been proposed
for P4 programs.
p4v

(
Liu
et al. 2018
)
applies classical
techniques based on predicate transformer semantics to achieve
monolithic verification of P4 programs. Vera
(
Stoenescu et al. 2018
)
and
P4-Assert
(
Freire et al. 2018
)
are symbolic execution engines for P4. The

bf4
tool
(
Dumitrescu et al. 2020
)
follows the approach of

p4v
, but also attempts to infer control-plane constraints
that are sufficiently strong to establish correctness, and offers
heuristics for repairing programs when verification fails.
SafeP4
(
Eichholz et al. 2019
)
uses a simple type system to track header
validity. Petr4
(
Doenges
et al. 2021
)
develops a formal semantics for P4
but does not itself offer verification tools. In constrast to this
earlier work,
\Pi
4 uses dependent types and offers compositional
verification.

Dependent Types.

Early work by

Xi and Pfenning 1999
showed
how dependent types could be used to eliminate run-time safety
checks—e.g., array bounds checks in imperative programs.
Xanadu
(
Xi 2000
)
adds dependent typing to imperative programming,
but does not capture the effect of mutations in the type.

Xi and Harper 2001

later showed how dependent types could be applied to assembly
code. Deputy
(
Condit et al. 2007
)
used dependent types to
reason about complex, heap-allocated data structures. Similar to
Deputy,
\Pi
4’s typing rule for modification of header fields is also
inspired by the Hoare axiom for assignment.
\Pi
4 is different in that
typechecking has no effect on the run time and also supports
path-sensitive reasoning. Similar to
\Pi
4, Hoare Type Theory
(HTT)
(
Nanevski
et al. 2006
)
statically tracks how the heap evolves
during execution. Typing of computations in HTT is similar to the
dependent function types
\Pi
4– the type captures
the state before and after execution, possibly relating the output
type with the input type. In our domain, this requires bit-by-bit
transformations on the input type, provided by
\mathsf{chomp}
. Other type
systems like Ynot
(
Nanevski et al. 2008
)
, FCSL
(
Nanevski et al. 2014
)
, and
F
∗

(
Swamy et al. 2016
)
provide dependent types for low-level
imperative programming.
They target general functional verification and often require manual
programs-as-proofs to do so.
\Pi
4 is designed with domain-specific
properties of network programming in mind and is fully automatic.

Solver-Aided Tools.

Recent work on dependently-typed languages has focused on
automation, building on advances in SAT/SMT solvers
to make dependent types usable by ordinary programs. A
prominent example
is Liquid Haskell
(
Rondon
et al. 2008
)
, which extends Haskell with
decidable refinement types.
Under the hood, proof obligations generated
during type checking are transparently handled by an SMT solver.
Just as Liquid Haskell requires its refinements to be in the theory of
quantifier-free integer linear arithmetic in order to be decidable,

\Pi
4 stipulates that types must denote finite sets—a restriction
justified by the domain. This assumption lets us encode types
into the effectively propositional fragment of first-order logic over bit
vectors.

Formalizing Protocols

Another line of work focuses on language-based specifications of protocols.
CMU’s FoxNet project used SML to specify the behavior of an entire networking
stack
(
Biagioni
et al. 1994
)
.

McCann and
Chandra 2000

used a type-based approach to give
abstract specifications of protocols. Grammar-based tools such
as PADS
(
Fisher and Gruber 2005
)
, Narcissus
(
Delaware et al. 2019
)
, and Yakker

(
Jim
et al. 2010
)
, enable specifying the syntax of complex, dependent formats
including network protocols, and provide tools for serializing and
deserializing data.
They focus exclusively on deriving correct
parsers from a typed representation of data and may be
suitable to describe the header formats and the parser, but there is no equivalent
to our chomp operator that allows us to statically capture how the input packet
changes during parsing.

8.
Conclusion and Future Work

This paper presented
\Pi
4—the first dependently-typed language for
data plane programming—a domain with difficult challenges where
programming language theory can have a big impact. In particular,
low-level data plane languages like P4 seem to be a sweet spot for
dependent types. On the one hand, precise types are necessary because
critical correctness properties often hinge on intricate, bit-level
packet formats, where the first few bits of a packet determine the
format, the length, and the processing of the following ones. On the
other hand, a high degree of automation is possible due to the
restricted nature of the language, which does not support pointers,
loops, or other features that often complicate very precise type
systems. Yet, thus far, dependent typing has not been explored for
data plane programs—the community has relied on verification tools
that lack compositional reasoning.

\Pi
4’s type system is innovative in its combination of refinement
types, dependent function types, a limited form of regular types,
including unions, explicit substitutions, and a primitive “chomp”
operation, reminiscent of regular expression
derivatives
(
Brzozowski 1964
)
, which can be used to give a precise
type to P4’s parsing constructs.
It is capable of statically checking advanced properties of data plane programs
that combine packet serialization and deserialization
operations with imperative control-flow.
Under the hood, an SMT solver automatically discharges the formulas
generated during type checking without requiring any manual proof. We
define
\Pi
4 formally and prove type soundness and decidability. Our
case studies demonstrate how
\Pi
4 supports modular reasoning in
scenarios ranging from basic safety properties to intricate
invariants.

There are a number of interesting directions for future work.
We plan to investigate connections that our verified approach to parsing
using derivatives may have to other domains, e.g., verified serializers and
deserializers like EverParse
(
Ramananandro et al. 2019
)
and Narcissus
(
Delaware et al. 2019
)
.
Another direction is to consider the effect of modularity on verification times;
if a tool incrementally caches verification results for ascribed code
blocks, it would only have to check the portions of the code that
change between runs of the typechecker. Some preliminary experiments
indicate that modular typechecking offers significant benefits, but an
empirical study to answer this question carefully is
left for future work, after we have optimized our prototype. We also plan to extend
\Pi
4 to handle more complicated features of P4
perhaps requiring concolic techniques
(
Godefroid
et al. 2005
)
. Further,
understanding whether dependent types are the right interface for
modular verification of dataplane programs is important. In fact, user
studies investigating the appropriate typing interfaces, such as
gradual typing and type inference, would be important for guiding the
design of impactful systems for modular data plane verification.

We are grateful to the POPL reviewers for their careful feedback and
many suggestions for improving this paper. Our work has been supported
in part by the German Research Foundation (DFG) as part of the Collaborative
Research Center (CRC) 1053 MAKI, by the National Research Center for Applied
Cybersecurity ATHENE, by the National Science Foundation under grant FMiTF-1918396
as well as a Graduate Research Fellowship, the Defense Advanced
Research Projects Agency under Contract HR001120C0107, and gifts from
Keysight and InfoSys.

\displaystyle\mathsf{chomp}_{1}

\displaystyle:\tau\times B_{n}\rightarrow\tau

\displaystyle\mathsf{chomp}_{1}(\Sigma x\!:\tau_{1}.\tau_{2},\mathsf{b_{n}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\Sigma x\!:\mathsf{chomp}_{1}(\tau_{1},\mathsf{b_{n}}).\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau_{2},x,\mathsf{b_{n}})~+

\displaystyle\phantom{{}\triangleq{}}\Sigma x\!:\{y:\tau_{1}\mid\lvert y.{pkt_{in}}\rvert=0\}.\mathsf{chomp}_{1}(\tau_{2},\mathsf{b_{n}})

\displaystyle\mathsf{chomp}_{1}(\tau_{1}+\tau_{2},\mathsf{b_{n}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{chomp}_{1}(\tau_{1},\mathsf{b_{n}})+\mathsf{chomp}_{1}(\tau_{2},b_{0})

\displaystyle\mathsf{chomp}_{1}(\{x:\tau\mid e\},\mathsf{b_{n}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\{x:\mathsf{chomp}_{1}(\tau,\mathsf{b_{n}})\mid\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(e,x,\mathsf{b_{n}})\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{chomp}_{1}(\tau_{1}[x\mapsto\tau_{2}],\mathsf{b_{n}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{chomp}_{1}(\tau_{1},\mathsf{b_{n}})[x\mapsto\tau_{2}]

\displaystyle\mathsf{chomp}_{1}(\tau,\_)

\displaystyle\triangleq\tau

\displaystyle\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}

\displaystyle:\tau\times\mathcal{X}\times B_{n}\rightarrow\tau

\displaystyle\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\Sigma x\!:\tau_{1}.\tau_{2},x,\mathsf{b_{n}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\Sigma x\!:\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau_{1},x,\mathsf{b_{n}}).\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau_{2},x,\mathsf{b_{n}})

\displaystyle\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau_{1}+\tau_{2},x,\mathsf{b_{n}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau_{1},x,\mathsf{b_{n}})+\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau_{2},x,\mathsf{b_{n}})

\displaystyle\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\{x:\tau\mid\varphi\},x,\mathsf{b_{n}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\{x:\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau,x,\mathsf{b_{n}})\mid\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(\varphi,x,\mathsf{b_{n}})\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau_{1}[y\mapsto\tau_{2}],x,\mathsf{b_{n}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau_{1},x,\mathsf{b_{n}})[y\mapsto\mathsf{chompRef}(\tau_{2},x,\mathsf{b_{n}})]

\displaystyle\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau,\_,\_)

\displaystyle\triangleq\tau

\displaystyle\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}

\displaystyle:\varphi\times\mathcal{X}\times B_{n}\rightarrow\varphi

\displaystyle\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(t_{1}=t_{2},x,\mathsf{b_{n}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(t_{1},x,\mathsf{b_{n}})=\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(t_{2},x,\mathsf{b_{n}})

\displaystyle\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(t_{1}>t_{2},x,\mathsf{b_{n}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(t_{1},x,\mathsf{b_{n}})>\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(t_{2},x,\mathsf{b_{n}})

\displaystyle\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2},x,\mathsf{b_{n}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(\varphi_{1},x,\mathsf{b_{n}})\land\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}(\varphi_{2},x,\mathsf{b_{n}})

\displaystyle\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(\neg\varphi,x,\mathsf{b_{n}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\neg\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(\varphi,x,\mathsf{b_{n}})

\displaystyle\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(\varphi,\_,\_)

\displaystyle\triangleq\varphi

\displaystyle\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}

\displaystyle:e\times\mathcal{X}\times B_{n}\rightarrow e

\displaystyle\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(\lvert x.{pkt_{in}}\rvert,y,\_)

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}\lvert x.{pkt_{in}}\rvert+1&\text{if }x=y\\
\lvert x.{pkt_{in}}\rvert&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(x.{pkt_{in}},y,\mathsf{b_{n}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}\langle\mathsf{b_{n}}\rangle@x.{pkt_{in}}&\text{if }x=y\\
x.{pkt_{in}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(x.{pkt_{in}}[l\!:\!r],y,\mathsf{b_{n}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}x.{pkt_{in}}[l\!:\!r]&\text{if }x\neq y\\
\langle\mathsf{b_{n}}\rangle&\text{if }x=y\land r\leq 1\\
\langle\mathsf{b_{n}}\rangle@x.{pkt_{in}}[0\!:\!r-1]&\text{if }x=y\land l=0\\
x.{pkt_{in}}[l-1\!:\!r-1]&\text{if }x=y\land l\neq 0\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(n+m,y,\mathsf{b_{n}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(n,y,\mathsf{b_{n}})+\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(m,y,\mathsf{b_{n}})

\displaystyle\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(bv_{1}@bv_{2},y,\mathsf{b_{n}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(bv_{1},y,\mathsf{b_{n}})@\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(bv_{2},y,\mathsf{b_{n}})

\displaystyle\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(e,\_,\_)

\displaystyle\triangleq e

\displaystyle\phantom{{}\triangleq\Sigma x:{}}\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\tau_{2},\mathsf{b_{n}},y,\iota,n)

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\tau_{1},\mathsf{b_{n}},y,\iota,n)+

\displaystyle\phantom{{}\triangleq{}}\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\tau_{2},\mathsf{b_{n}},y,\iota,n)

\displaystyle\phantom{{}\triangleq\{x:{}}\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\varphi,\mathsf{b_{n}},y,\iota,n)\}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\tau_{2},\mathsf{b_{n}},y,\iota,n)]

\displaystyle\triangleq\tau

Additional Rules for the Operational Semantics

\frac{\langle I,O,H,\varphi\rangle\rightarrow\varphi^{\prime}}{\langle I,O,H,\mathit{if(\varphi)\ then\ c_{1}\ else\ c_{2}}\rangle\rightarrow\\
\langle I,O,H,\mathit{if(\varphi^{\prime})\ then\ c_{1}\ else\ c_{2}}\rangle}\quad\textsc{E-If}\frac{\ }{\langle I,O,H,\mathit{if(\mathsf{true})\ then\ c_{1}\ else\ c_{2}}\rangle\rightarrow\langle I,O,H,c_{1}\rangle}\quad\textsc{E-IfTrue}\frac{\ }{\langle I,O,H,\mathit{if(\mathsf{false})\ then\ c_{1}\ else\ c_{2}}\rangle\rightarrow\langle I,O,H,c_{2}\rangle}\quad\textsc{E-IfFalse}\frac{\ }{\langle I,O,H,\mathit{skip};c_{2}\rangle\rightarrow\langle I,O,H,c_{2}\rangle}\quad\textsc{E-Seq}\frac{\langle I,O,H,c_{1}\rangle\rightarrow\langle I^{\prime},O^{\prime},H^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime}\rangle}{\langle I,O,H,c_{1};c_{2}\rangle\rightarrow\langle I^{\prime},O^{\prime},H^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime};c_{2}\rangle}\quad\textsc{E-Seq1}\frac{\langle I,O,H,e\rangle\rightarrow e^{\prime}}{\langle I,O,H,\iota.f=e\rangle\rightarrow\langle I,O,H,\iota.f:=e^{\prime}\rangle}\quad\textsc{E-Mod1}

Appendix B
Safety

We assume that the environment
\mathcal{E}
is empty whenever it is omitted from the entailment relation or the semantics of expressions and formulae.
In the context of the semantics of heap types, we use
(I,O,H)
as a shorthand notation for the heap
H[{pkt_{in}}\mapsto I,pkt_{out}\mapsto O]
.
For example, we write
(I,O,H)\in\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
instead of
H[{pkt_{in}}\mapsto I,pkt_{out}\mapsto O]\in\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
.

Lemma B.1 (Semantic Entailment).

If
(I,O,H)\models_{\mathcal{E}}\tau
, then
(I,O,H)\in\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}

Proof.

By induction on
\tau
.

Case

\tau=\varnothing
::

Immediate, since
(I,O,H)\models_{\mathcal{E}}\varnothing
is a contradiction.

Case

\tau=\top
::

Immediate, since
\llbracket\top\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}=\mathcal{H}
.

Case

\tau=\Sigma x\!:\tau_{1}.\tau_{2}
::

By inversion of entailment, we get

(A1):

(I,O,H)=(I_{1}@I_{2},O_{1}@O_{2},H_{1}\cup H_{2})
and

(A2):

(I_{1},O_{1},H_{1})\models_{\mathcal{E}}\tau_{1}
and

(A3):

(I_{2},O_{2},H_{2})\models_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto(I_{1},O_{1},H_{1})]}\tau_{2}

By
(A2)
respectively
(A3)
and the induction hypothesis, we get

(A4):

(I_{1},O_{1},H_{1})\in\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
and

(A5):

(I_{2},O_{2},H_{2})\in\llbracket\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto(I_{1},O_{1},H_{1})]}
.

To show that
(I,O,H)\in\llbracket\Sigma x\!:\tau_{1}.\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}=\{h_{1}+\!\!+\ h_{2}\mid h_{1}\in\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}\land h_{2}\in\llbracket\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h_{1}]}\}
,
we have to show that
(I,O,H)
is the concatenation of two heaps
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
, where
h_{1}\in\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
and

h_{2}\in\llbracket\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h_{1}]}
, which follows from
(A1)
,
(A4)
and
(A5)
.

Case

\tau=\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}
::

By inversion of entailment, either
(I,O,H)\models_{\mathcal{E}}\tau_{1}
or
(I,O,H)\models_{\mathcal{E}}\tau_{2}
.
To show that
(I,O,H)\in\llbracket\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}=\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}\cup\llbracket\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
,
we have to show that
(I,O,H)\in\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
or
(I,O,H)\in\llbracket\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
.

Subcase

(I,O,H)\models_{\mathcal{E}}\tau_{1}
::

By induction hypothesis,
(I,O,H)\in\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
.
We can conclude
(I,O,H)\in\llbracket\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
.

Subcase

(I,O,H)\models_{\mathcal{E}}\tau_{2}
::

Symmetric to previous subcase.

Case

\tau=\{x:\tau_{1}\mid\varphi\}
::

By inversion of entailment, we get

(A1):

(I,O,H)\models_{\mathcal{E}}\tau
and

(A2):

\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto(I,O,H)]}=true

To show that
(I,O,H)\in\llbracket\{x:\tau\mid\varphi\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}=\{h\mid h\in\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}\land\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h]}\}
, we have to show that
(I,O,H)\in\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
and that
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto(I,O,H)]}=true
.
The first follows by induction hypothesis and
(A1)
and the latter by
(A2)
.

Case

\tau=\tau_{1}{[x\mapsto\tau_{2}]}
::

By inversion of entailment, we get

(A1):

(I_{2},O_{2},H_{2})\models_{\mathcal{E}}\tau_{2}
for some
I_{2},O_{2},H_{2}
and

(A2):

(I,O,H)\models_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto(I_{2},O_{2},H_{2})]}\tau_{1}

To show that
(I,O,H)\in\llbracket\tau_{1}[x\mapsto\tau_{2}]\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}=\{h\mid h_{2}\in\llbracket\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}\land h\in\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h_{2}]}\}
, we have to show that
(I,O,H)\in\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h_{2}]}
where
h_{2}\in\llbracket\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
.
By induction hypothesis and
(A2)
follows that
(I,O,H)\in\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto(I_{2},O_{2},H_{2})]}
.

(I_{2},O_{2},H_{2})\in\llbracket\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
follows by induction hypothesis and
(A1)
, which concludes this case.

∎

Lemma B.2 (Semantic Containment Entails).

If
(I,O,H)\in\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
, then
(I,O,H)\models_{\mathcal{E}}\tau
.

Proof.

By induction on
\tau
.

Case

\tau=\varnothing
::

Immediate, since there is no heap in
\llbracket\varnothing\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
.

Case

\tau=\top
::

Result directly follows by
Ent-Top
.

Case

\tau=\Sigma x\!:\tau_{1}.\tau_{2}
::

By the semantics of heap types, all heaps
h\in\llbracket\Sigma x\!:\tau_{1}.\tau_{2}\rrbracket
have the form
h=h_{1}+\!\!+\ h_{2}
, where
h_{1}=(I_{1},O_{1},H_{1})\in\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
and
h_{2}=(I_{2},O_{2},H_{2})\in\llbracket\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h1]}
.
By applying the induction hypothesis, we get
(I_{1},O_{1},H_{1})\models_{\mathcal{E}}\tau_{1}
and
(I_{2},O_{2},H_{2})\models_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h_{1}]}\tau_{2}
.
The result directly follows by
Ent-Sigma
.

Case

\tau=\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}
::

By the semantics of heap types, for any
h\in\llbracket\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
holds that either
h\in\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
or
h\in\llbracket\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
.

Subcase

h\in\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
::

By induction hypothesis,
h\models_{\mathcal{E}}\tau_{1}
. The result directly follows by
Ent-ChoiceL
.

Subcase

h\in\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
::

Symmetric to previous subcase.

Case

\tau=\{y:\tau_{1}\mid\varphi\}
::

By the semantics of heap types,
h\in\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
and
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h]}=\mathit{true}
.
By induction hypothesis,
h\models_{\mathcal{E}}\tau
.
The result directly follows by
Ent-Refine
.

Case

\tau=\tau_{1}{[x\mapsto\tau_{2}]}
::

By the semantics of heap types,
h\in\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h_{2}]}
where
h_{2}\in\llbracket\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
.
By induction hypothesis,
h_{2}\models_{\mathcal{E}}\tau_{2}
and
h\models_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h_{2}]}\tau_{1}
.
The result directly follows by
Ent-Subst
.

∎

Lemma B.3 (Subtype Entailment).

If
(I,O,H)\models_{\mathcal{E}}\tau_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}\models\Gamma
and
\Gamma\vdash\tau_{1}<:\tau_{2}
, then
(I,O,H)\models_{\mathcal{E}}\tau_{2}
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

B.1
,
(I,O,H)\in\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
.
With
\mathcal{E}\models\Gamma
and by definition of subtyping,

(I,O,H)\in\llbracket\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
.
The result follows by
Lemma

B.2
.
∎

Lemma B.4 (Extended Environment Entails).

If
\mathcal{E}\models\Gamma
and
(I,O,H)\models_{\mathcal{E}}\tau
and
x\not\in\mathit{dom}(\mathcal{E})
, then
\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto(I,O,H)]\models\Gamma,x:\tau
.

Proof.

By definition of entailment between environments and typing contexts and by assumptions.
∎

Lemma B.5 (Included Instances in Domain).

If
(I,O,H)\models_{\mathcal{E}}\tau
and
\mathsf{Includes}~\Gamma~\tau~\iota
, then
\iota\in\mathit{dom}(H)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
B.1
,
(I,O,H)\in\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
.
By assumption
\mathsf{Includes}~\Gamma~\tau~\iota
and by definition of inclusion,
\forall h\in\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}.\iota\in\mathit{dom}(h)
, we can conclude that
\iota\in\mathit{dom}(H)
.
∎

Lemma B.6 (Excluded Instances not in Domain).

If
(I,O,H)\models_{\mathcal{E}}\tau
and
\mathsf{Excludes}~\Gamma~\tau~\iota
, then
\iota\not\in\mathit{dom}(H)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
B.1
,
(I,O,H)\in\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
.
By assumption
\mathsf{Excludes}~\Gamma~\tau~\iota
and by definition of exclusion,
\forall h\in\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}.\iota\not\in\mathit{dom}(h)
, we can conclude that
\iota\not\in\mathit{dom}(H)
.
∎

Lemma B.7 (Expression Progress).

If
\Gamma;\tau\vdash e:\sigma
and

\mathcal{E}\models\Gamma
and

(I,O,H)\models_{\mathcal{E}}\tau
,
then either
e
is a value or

\exists e^{\prime}.\langle I,O,H,e\rangle\rightarrow e^{\prime}
.

Lemma B.8 (Formulae Progress).

If
\Gamma;\tau\vdash\varphi:\mathbb{B}
and
\mathcal{E}\models\Gamma
and
(I,O,H)\models_{\mathcal{E}}\tau
,
then either
\varphi
is a value or
\exists\varphi^{\prime}.\langle I,O,H,\varphi\rangle\rightarrow\varphi^{\prime}

Theorem B.9 (Progress).

If
\Gamma\vdash c\!:\!(x:\tau_{1})\rightarrow\tau_{2}
and
\mathcal{E}\models\Gamma
and
(I,O,H)\models_{\mathcal{E}}\tau_{1}
, then either
c=\mathit{skip}
or
there exists
\langle I^{\prime},O^{\prime},H^{\prime},c^{\prime}\rangle
such that
\langle I,O,H,c\rangle\rightarrow\langle I^{\prime},O^{\prime},H^{\prime},c^{\prime}\rangle
.

Proof.

By induction on typing derivations of
\Gamma\vdash c\!:\!(x:\tau_{1})\rightarrow\tau_{2}
.

Case

T-Skip
::

c=\mathit{skip}

The result is immediate.

Case

T-Extract
::

c=\mathit{extract(\iota)}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{sizeof}_{{pkt_{in}}}(\tau_{1})\geq\mathsf{sizeof}(\iota)

By inversion of
(I,O,H)\models_{\mathcal{E}}\tau_{1}
, we know that
I
contains enough bits such that
\mathit{deserialize}_{\eta}(I)
does not fail.
Let
(v,I^{\prime})=\mathit{deserialize_{\eta}(I)}
and
O^{\prime}=O
and
H^{\prime}=H[\iota\mapsto v]
and
c^{\prime}=\mathit{skip}
.
The result follows by
E-Extract
.

Case

T-Reset
::

c=\mathit{reset}

Let
I^{\prime}=O@I,O^{\prime}=\langle\rangle,H^{\prime}=[]
and
c^{\prime}=\mathit{skip}
.
The result follows by
E-Reset
.

Case

T-Remit
::

c=\mathit{remit(\iota)}
and

\mathsf{Includes}~\Gamma~\tau_{1}~\iota

By
Lemma

B.5
we know
\iota\in dom(H)
.
Let
I^{\prime}=I,O^{\prime}=O@\mathit{serialize_{\eta}}(H(\iota)),H^{\prime}=H
and
c^{\prime}=\mathit{skip}
.
The result follows by
E-Remit

Case

T-Mod
::

c=\iota.f:=e
and

\mathsf{Includes}~\Gamma~\tau_{1}~\iota
and

\mathcal{F}(\iota,f)=\mathsf{BV}
and

\Gamma;\tau_{1}\vdash e:\mathsf{BV}
and

\tau_{2}=\{y:\top\mid\varphi_{pkt}\wedge\varphi_{\iota}\wedge\varphi_{f}\wedge y.\iota.f=e[x/\mathsf{heap}]\}

By
Lemma

B.7
, either
e
is a value
or there is some
e^{\prime}
such that
\langle I,O,H,e\rangle\rightarrow e^{\prime}
.

Subcase

e=v
::

By
Lemma

B.5
,
\iota\in dom(H)
.
Let
r=H(\iota)
and
r^{\prime}=\{r\textit{ with }f=v\}
.
Let
I^{\prime}=I,O^{\prime}=O,H^{\prime}=H[\iota\mapsto r]
and
c^{\prime}=\mathit{skip}
. The result follows by
E-Mod
.

Subcase

\langle I,O,H,e\rangle\rightarrow e^{\prime}
::

Let
I^{\prime}=I,O^{\prime}=O,H^{\prime}=H^{\prime}
and
c^{\prime}=\iota.f:=t^{\prime}
.
The result follows by
E-Mod1
.

Case

T-Seq
::

c=c_{1};c_{2}
and

\Gamma\vdash c_{1}\!:\!(x:\tau_{1})\rightarrow\tau_{1}^{\prime}
and

\Gamma\vdash c_{2}\!:\!(x:\tau_{1}^{\prime})\rightarrow\tau_{2}

By IH,
c_{1}
is either
\mathit{skip}
or there is some
\langle I^{\prime},O^{\prime},H^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime}\rangle
, such that
\langle I,O,H,c_{1}\rangle\rightarrow\langle I^{\prime},O^{\prime},H^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime}\rangle
.
If
c_{1}=\mathit{skip}
, let
I^{\prime}=I,O^{\prime}=O,H^{\prime}=H
and
c^{\prime}=c_{2}
.

The result follows by
E-Seq
.
Otherwise, the result follows by
E-Seq1
.

Case

T-If
::

c=\mathit{if(\varphi)\ then\ c_{1}\ else\ c_{2}}
and

\Gamma;\tau_{1}\vdash e:\mathbb{B}

By
Lemma

B.8
, we have that
\varphi
is either
\mathsf{true},\mathsf{false}
or there is some
\varphi^{\prime}
such that
\langle I,O,H,\varphi\rangle\rightarrow\varphi^{\prime}
.

Subcase

\varphi=\mathsf{true}
::

Let
I^{\prime}=I,O^{\prime}=O,H^{\prime}=H
and
c^{\prime}=c_{1}
. The result follows by
E-IfTrue
.

Subcase

\varphi=\mathsf{false}
::

Symmetric to previous subcase.

Subcase

\langle I,O,H,\varphi\rangle\rightarrow\varphi^{\prime}
::

Let
I^{\prime}=I
and
O^{\prime}=O
and
H^{\prime}=H
.

Further, let
c^{\prime}=\mathit{if(\varphi^{\prime})\ then\ c_{1}\ else\ c_{2}}
.
The result follows by
E-If
.

Case

T-Add
::

c=\mathit{add}{\iota}
and

\mathsf{Excludes}~\Gamma~\tau~\iota
.

By
Lemma

B.6
,
\iota\not\in\mathit{dom}(H)
.
The result follows by
E-Add
.

Case

T-Ascribe
::

c=c_{a}\ \mathit{as}\ (x:\tau_{a_{1}})\rightarrow\tau_{a_{2}}
.
Let
I^{\prime}=I
,
O^{\prime}=O
,
H^{\prime}=H
and
c^{\prime}=c_{a}
.
The result follows by
E-Ascribe
.

Case

T-Sub
::

\Gamma\vdash\tau_{1}<:\tau_{3}
and

\Gamma,x:\tau_{1}\vdash\tau_{4}<:\tau_{2}
and

\Gamma\vdash c:(x:\tau_{3})\rightarrow\tau_{4}
.
By
Lemma

B.3
,
(I,O,H)\models_{\mathcal{E}}\tau_{3}
.
By IH,
c=\mathit{skip}
or there exists
I^{\prime},O^{\prime},H^{\prime},c^{\prime}
s.t.

\langle I,O,H,c\rangle\rightarrow\langle I^{\prime},O^{\prime},H^{\prime},c^{\prime}\rangle
.
The result follows directly.

∎

Lemma B.10 (Weakening).

If
\Gamma\vdash c\!:\!(x:\tau_{1})\to\tau_{2}
and variable
z
does not appear free in
\tau_{1}
or
\tau_{2}
, then

\Gamma,z:\tau\vdash c\!:\!(x:\tau_{1})\to\tau_{2}
for any heap type
\tau
.

Proof.

By induction on the typing derivation.
∎

Lemma B.11 (Input Type Strengthening).

If
\Gamma\vdash c:(x:\tau_{1})\rightarrow\tau_{2}
and

\llbracket\tau_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}\subseteq\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
and

\mathcal{E}\models\Gamma
and

\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\models\Gamma^{\prime}
and

\Gamma\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}
and

\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
, then

\exists\tau_{2}^{\prime}.\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash c:(x:\tau_{1}^{\prime})\rightarrow\tau_{2}^{\prime}
and

\forall h^{\prime}\in\llbracket\tau_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}.\llbracket\tau_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h^{\prime}]}\subseteq\llbracket\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

Proof.

By induction on a derivation of
\Gamma\vdash c:(x:\tau_{1})\rightarrow\tau_{2}
with case analysis on the last rule used.
We refer to the proof goals as follows:

(1)

\exists\tau_{2}^{\prime}.\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash c:(x:\tau_{1}^{\prime})\rightarrow\tau_{2}^{\prime}

(2)

\forall h^{\prime}\in\llbracket\tau_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}.\llbracket\tau_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h^{\prime}]}\subseteq\llbracket\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

We refer to the assumptions as follows:

(A)

\llbracket\tau_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}\subseteq\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}

(B)

\mathcal{E}\models\Gamma

(C)

\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\models\Gamma^{\prime}

(D)

\Gamma\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}

(E)

\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{\prime}

Case

T-Add
::

By inversion of rule
T-Add
, we get

(A1):

\mathsf{Excludes}~\Gamma~\tau~\iota
and

(A2):

init_{\mathcal{HT}(\iota)}=v

(A3):

\tau_{2}=\Sigma y\!:\{z:\tau_{1}\mid z\equiv x\}.\{z:\iota\mid z.pkt_{out}=z.{pkt_{in}}=\langle\rangle\wedge z.\iota=v\}
.

Let
\tau_{2}^{\prime}=\Sigma y\!:\{z:\tau_{1}^{\prime}\mid z\equiv x\}.\{z:\iota\mid z.pkt_{out}=z.{pkt_{in}}=\langle\rangle\wedge z.\iota=v\}
.
By assumptions
(A1)
,
(A)
and
(C)
we can conclude that
\mathsf{Excludes}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}~\tau_{1}^{\prime}~\iota
must also hold.

(1)
follows by
T-Add
.

Let
h^{\prime}\in\llbracket\tau_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
be arbitrary.

\displaystyle\llbracket\Sigma y\!:\{z:\tau_{1}^{\prime}\mid z\equiv x\}.\{z:\iota\mid z.pkt_{out}=z.{pkt_{in}}=\langle\rangle\wedge z.\iota=v\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h^{\prime}]}\subseteq

\displaystyle\llbracket\Sigma y\!:\{z:\tau_{1}\mid z\equiv x\}.\{z:\iota\mid z.pkt_{out}=z.{pkt_{in}}=\langle\rangle\wedge z.\iota=v\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\{h_{1}+\!\!+\ h_{2}\mid h_{1}\in\llbracket\{z:\tau_{1}^{\prime}\mid z\equiv x\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h^{\prime}]}\ \wedge

\displaystyle\hskip 46.0pth_{2}\in\llbracket\{z:\iota\mid z.pkt_{out}=z.{pkt_{in}}=\langle\rangle\wedge z.\iota=v\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h^{\prime},y\mapsto h_{1}]}\}\subseteq

\displaystyle\{h_{1}+\!\!+\ h_{2}\mid h_{1}\in\llbracket\{z:\tau_{1}\mid z\equiv x\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h^{\prime}]}\ \wedge

\displaystyle\hskip 46.0pth_{2}\in\llbracket\{z:\iota\mid z.pkt_{out}=z.{pkt_{in}}=\langle\rangle\wedge z.\iota=v\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h^{\prime},y\mapsto h_{1}]}\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\{h^{\prime}+\!\!+\ h_{2}\mid h_{2}\in\llbracket\{z:\iota\mid z.pkt_{out}=z.{pkt_{in}}=\langle\rangle\wedge z.\iota=v\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h^{\prime},y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}\}\subseteq

\displaystyle\{h^{\prime}+\!\!+\ h_{2}\mid h_{2}\in\llbracket\{z:\iota\mid z.pkt_{out}=z.{pkt_{in}}=\langle\rangle\wedge z.\iota=v\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h^{\prime},y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}\}

The type
\{z:\iota\mid z.pkt_{out}=z.{pkt_{in}}=\langle\rangle\wedge z.\iota=v\}
does not contain any free variables, so the semantics does not depend on the environment.
In fact, the sets of heaps described by
\tau_{2}^{\prime}
and
\tau_{2}
is actually equivalent, which shows
(2)
.

Case

T-Ascribe
::

By inversion of rule
T-Ascribe
, we get

(A1):

c=c_{a}\ \mathit{as}\ (x:\tau_{1})\to\tau_{2}
and

(A2):

\Gamma\vdash c_{a}\!:\!(x:\tau_{1})\to\tau_{2}

From assumptions
(A2)
and
(D)
together with
Lemma

B.10
follows that

(A3):

\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash c_{a}\!:\!(x:\tau_{1})\to\tau_{2}

Since
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
differs from
\mathcal{E}
only in that it potentially contains additional bindings, we can conclude that

(A4):

\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}=\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
and together with assumption
(B)

(A5):

\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\tau_{1}^{\prime}<:\tau_{1}
.

By assumption
(A3)
and
T-Ascribe
we get

(A6):

\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash c_{a}\ as\ (x:\tau_{1})\to\tau_{2}\!:\!(x:\tau_{1})\to\tau_{2}

Let
\tau_{2}^{\prime}=\tau_{2}
.

(1)
follows by
T-Sub
.

For
(2)
, we have to show that
\forall h^{\prime}\in\llbracket\tau_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}.\llbracket\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h^{\prime}]}\subseteq\llbracket\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h^{\prime}]}
.
In fact
\tau_{2}
describes the same set of heaps, both in
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
.
Variable
x
binds to the same heap and both environments provide the same bindings for any other free variable in
\tau_{2}
.

Case

T-Extract
::

By inversion of rule
T-Extract
, we get

(A1):

\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{sizeof}_{pkt_{in}}(\tau_{1})\geq\mathsf{sizeof}(\iota)

(A2):

\tau_{2}=\Sigma y\!:\{z:\iota\mid z.{pkt_{in}}=z.pkt_{out}=\langle\rangle\}.\{z:\mathsf{chomp}(\tau_{1},\iota,y)\mid\\
y.\iota@z.{pkt_{in}}=x.{pkt_{in}}\wedge z.pkt_{out}=x.pkt_{out}\wedge z\equiv_{\iota}x\}

Let
\tau_{2}^{\prime}=\Sigma y\!:\{z:\iota\mid z.{pkt_{in}}=z.pkt_{out}=\langle\rangle\}.\{z:\mathsf{chomp}(\tau_{1}^{\prime},\iota,y)\mid\\
y.\iota@z.{pkt_{in}}=x.{pkt_{in}}\wedge z.pkt_{out}=x.pkt_{out}\wedge z\equiv_{\iota}x\}
.
By assumptions
(A)
and
(A1)
follows that
\mathsf{sizeof}_{pkt_{in}}(\tau_{1}^{\prime})\geq\mathsf{sizeof}(\iota)

(1)
follows by
T-Extract
.

Let
h^{\prime}\in\llbracket\tau_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
be arbitrary.

\displaystyle\llbracket\Sigma y\!:\{z:\iota\mid z.{pkt_{in}}=z.pkt_{out}=\langle\rangle\}.

\displaystyle\hskip 23.0pt\{z:\mathsf{chomp}(\tau_{1}^{\prime},\iota,y)\mid y.\iota@z.{pkt_{in}}=x.{pkt_{in}}\ \wedge

\displaystyle\hskip 109.0ptz.pkt_{out}=x.pkt_{out}\wedge z\equiv_{\iota}x\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h^{\prime}]}\subseteq

\displaystyle\llbracket\Sigma y\!:\{z:\iota\mid z.{pkt_{in}}=z.pkt_{out}=\langle\rangle\}.

\displaystyle\hskip 23.0pt\{z:\mathsf{chomp}(\tau_{1},\iota,y)\mid y.\iota@z.{pkt_{in}}=x.{pkt_{in}}\ \wedge

\displaystyle\hskip 109.0ptz.pkt_{out}=x.pkt_{out}\wedge z\equiv_{\iota}x\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\{h_{1}+\!\!+\ h_{2}\mid h_{1}\in\llbracket\{z:\iota\mid z.{pkt_{in}}=z.pkt_{out}=\langle\rangle\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h^{\prime}]}\ \wedge

\displaystyle\hskip 46.0pth_{2}\in\llbracket\{z:\mathsf{chomp}(\tau_{1}^{\prime},\iota,y)\mid y.\iota@z.{pkt_{in}}=x.{pkt_{in}}\ \wedge

\displaystyle\hskip 156.0ptz.pkt_{out}=x.pkt_{out}\ \wedge

\displaystyle\hskip 156.0ptz\equiv_{\iota}x\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h^{\prime},y\mapsto h_{1}]}\}\subseteq

\displaystyle\{h_{1}+\!\!+\ h_{2}\mid h_{1}\in\llbracket\{z:\iota\mid z.{pkt_{in}}=z.pkt_{out}=\langle\rangle\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h^{\prime}]}\ \wedge

\displaystyle\hskip 46.0pth_{2}\in\llbracket\{z:\mathsf{chomp}(\tau_{1},\iota,y)\mid y.\iota@z.{pkt_{in}}=x.{pkt_{in}}\ \wedge

\displaystyle\hskip 156.0ptz.pkt_{out}=x.pkt_{out}\ \wedge

\displaystyle\hskip 156.0ptz\equiv_{\iota}x\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h^{\prime},y\mapsto h_{1}]}\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\{h_{1}+\!\!+\ h_{2}\mid h_{1}\in\llbracket\{z:\iota\mid z.{pkt_{in}}=z.pkt_{out}=\langle\rangle\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h^{\prime}]}\ \wedge

\displaystyle\hskip 46.0pth_{2}\in\{h_{22}|h_{22}\in\llbracket\mathsf{chomp}(\tau_{1}^{\prime},\iota,y)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h^{\prime},y\mapsto h_{1}]}\ \wedge

\displaystyle\hskip 107.0pt\llbracket y.\iota@z.{pkt_{in}}=x.{pkt_{in}}\ \wedge z.pkt_{out}=x.pkt_{out}\ \wedge

\displaystyle\hskip 112.0ptz\equiv_{\iota}x\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h^{\prime},y\mapsto h_{1},z\mapsto h_{22}]}\}\}\subseteq

\displaystyle\{h_{1}+\!\!+\ h_{2}\mid h_{1}\in\llbracket\{z:\iota\mid z.{pkt_{in}}=z.pkt_{out}=\langle\rangle\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h^{\prime}]}\ \wedge

\displaystyle\hskip 46.0pth_{2}\in\{h_{22}|h_{22}\in\llbracket\mathsf{chomp}(\tau_{1},\iota,y)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h^{\prime},y\mapsto h_{1}]}\ \wedge

\displaystyle\hskip 107.0pt\llbracket y.\iota@z.{pkt_{in}}=x.{pkt_{in}}\ \wedge z.pkt_{out}=x.pkt_{out}\ \wedge

\displaystyle\hskip 112.0ptz\equiv_{\iota}x\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h^{\prime},y\mapsto h_{1},z\mapsto h_{22}]}\}\}

By
Lemma

B.20
, we obtain all heaps contained in
\llbracket\mathsf{chomp}(\tau_{1}^{\prime},\iota,y)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h^{\prime},y\mapsto h_{1}]}
by taking all heaps from
\llbracket\tau_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
and removing the first
\mathsf{sizeof}(\iota)
bits from the input packet.
From assumption
(A)
we know that all heaps described by
\tau_{1}^{\prime}
are also contained in the set of heaps described by
\tau_{1}
and when we remove the first
\mathsf{sizeof}(\iota)
bits from the input packet, the relation still holds.
Since the rest of the types are identical this also holds for the concatenated heaps.
This shows
(2)
and concludes the case.

Case

T-If
::

By inversion of rule
T-If
, we get

(A1):

\Gamma;\tau_{1}\vdash e:\mathbb{B}

(A2):

\Gamma\vdash c_{1}:(x:\{y:\tau_{1}\mid\varphi[y/\mathsf{heap}]\})\to\tau_{12}

(A3):

\Gamma\vdash c_{2}:(x:\{y:\tau_{1}\mid\neg\varphi[y/\mathsf{heap}]\})\to\tau_{22}

(A4):

\tau_{2}=\{y:\tau_{12}\mid\varphi[x/\mathsf{heap}]\}+\{y:\tau_{22}\mid\varphi[x/\mathsf{heap}]\}

(A5):

c=\mathit{if(\varphi)\ then\ c_{1}\ else\ c_{2}}

To be able to conclude (1) by
T-If
, we must show that

(1.1):

\Gamma^{\prime};\tau_{1}^{\prime}\vdash\varphi:\mathbb{B}

(1.2):

\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash c_{1}:(x:\{y:\tau_{1}^{\prime}\mid\varphi[y/\mathsf{heap}]\})\to\tau_{12}^{\prime}

(1.3):

\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash c_{2}:(x:\{y:\tau_{1}^{\prime}\mid\neg\varphi[y/\mathsf{heap}]\})\to\tau_{22}^{\prime}

To apply the IH to
c_{1}
, we need some
\tau^{\prime}_{IH_{1}}
such that

\llbracket\tau^{\prime}_{IH_{1}}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}\subseteq\llbracket\{y:\tau_{1}\mid\varphi[y/\mathsf{heap}]\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}

Let
\tau^{\prime}_{IH_{1}}=\{y:\tau_{1}^{\prime}\mid\varphi[y/\mathsf{heap}]\}
.

By IH, there exists
\tau_{12}^{\prime}
such that

(A6):

\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash c_{1}:(x:\{y:\tau_{1}^{\prime}\mid\varphi[y/\mathsf{heap}]\})\to\tau_{12}^{\prime}

(A7):

\forall h_{1}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\{y:\tau_{1}^{\prime}\mid\varphi[y/\mathsf{heap}]\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}.\llbracket\tau_{12}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}\subseteq\llbracket\tau_{12}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}

With a similar argument as before, also by IH, there exists
\tau_{22}^{\prime}
such that

(A8):

\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash c_{2}:(x:\{y:\tau_{1}^{\prime}\mid\neg\varphi[y/\mathsf{heap}]\})\to\tau_{22}^{\prime}

(A9):

\forall h_{2}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\{y:\tau_{1}^{\prime}\mid\neg\varphi[y/\mathsf{heap}]\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}.\llbracket\tau_{22}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h_{2}^{\prime}]}=\llbracket\tau_{22}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h_{2}^{\prime}]}

\Gamma^{\prime};\tau_{1}^{\prime}\vdash\varphi:\mathbb{B}
also holds, because the subtyping relation between
\tau_{1}^{\prime}
and
\tau_{1}
ensures that heaps described by
\tau_{1}^{\prime}
have the same shape (i.e., the same instances are valid) and thus we can typecheck formula
e
in the context of type
\tau_{1}^{\prime}
.

(1)
follows by
T-If
.

Let
\tau_{2}^{\prime}=\{y:\tau_{12}^{\prime}\mid\varphi[x/\mathsf{heap}]\}+\{y:\tau_{22}^{\prime}\mid\neg\varphi[x/\mathsf{heap}]\}

To show
\forall h_{1}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\tau_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}.\llbracket\{y:\tau_{12}^{\prime}\mid\varphi[x/\mathsf{heap}]\}+\{y:\tau_{22}\mid\neg\varphi[x/\mathsf{heap}]\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}\subseteq\llbracket\{y:\tau_{12}\mid\varphi[x/\mathsf{heap}]\}+\{y:\tau_{22}\mid\neg\varphi[x/\mathsf{heap}]\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}

Let
h_{1}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\tau_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
be arbitrary.
Case distinction on wether the formula
\varphi
in
h_{1}^{\prime}
evaluates to true or false.

Subcase

e
evaluates to true::

\displaystyle\llbracket\{y:\tau_{12}^{\prime}\mid\varphi[x/\mathsf{heap}]\}+\{y:\tau_{22}\mid\neg\varphi[x/\mathsf{heap}]\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}\subseteq

\displaystyle\llbracket\{y:\tau_{12}\mid\varphi[x/\mathsf{heap}]\}+\{y:\tau_{22}\mid\neg\varphi[x/\mathsf{heap}]\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\llbracket\{y:\tau_{12}^{\prime}\mid\mathsf{true}\}+\{y:\tau_{22}\mid\mathsf{false}\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}\subseteq

\displaystyle\llbracket\{y:\tau_{12}\mid\mathsf{true}\}+\{y:\tau_{22}\mid\mathsf{false}\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\llbracket\tau_{12}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}\subseteq\llbracket\tau_{12}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}

The result follows by
(A7)
.

Subcase

e
evaluates to false::

Symmetric to previous subcase. The result follows by
(A9)
.

Case

T-Mod
::

(A1):

\tau_{2}=\{y:\top\mid\varphi_{pkt}\wedge\varphi_{\iota}\wedge\varphi_{f}\wedge y.\iota.f=e[x/\mathsf{heap}]\}

(A2):

\mathsf{Includes}~\Gamma~\tau_{1}~\iota

(A3):

\Gamma;\tau\vdash t:\mathsf{BV}

(A4):

\mathcal{F}(\iota,f)=\mathsf{BV}

(A5):

c=\iota.f:=e

To show: There exists
\tau_{2}^{\prime}
such that

(1):

\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\iota.f:=e:(x:\tau_{1}^{\prime})\to\tau_{2}^{\prime}
and

(2):

\forall h_{1}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\tau_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}.\llbracket\tau_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}\subseteq\llbracket\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}

Let
\tau_{2}^{\prime}=\tau_{2}
.

\mathsf{Includes}~\Gamma~\tau_{1}^{\prime}~\iota
follows by assumptions
(A2)
and
(A)
and set theory.
By assumption
(A)
, we know that
\tau_{1}^{\prime}
has the same shape (contains the same instances) as
\tau_{1}
,
so we can typecheck expression
e
in context
\tau_{1}^{\prime}
with a bit vector type,
from which follows that
\Gamma^{\prime};\tau_{1}^{\prime}\vdash e:\mathsf{BV}
.

(1)
follows by
T-Mod
.

Let
h_{1}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\tau_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
be arbitrary.
To show
(2)
, we must show that

\displaystyle\llbracket\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}\subseteq\llbracket\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\llbracket\{y:\top\mid\varphi_{pkt}\wedge\varphi_{\iota}\wedge\varphi_{f}\wedge y.\iota.f=t[x/\mathsf{heap}]\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}\subseteq

\displaystyle\llbracket\{y:\top\mid\varphi_{pkt}\wedge\varphi_{\iota}\wedge\varphi_{f}\wedge y.\iota.f=t[x/\mathsf{heap}]\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}

Since the only free variable is
x

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\llbracket\{y:\top\mid\varphi_{pkt}\wedge\varphi_{\iota}\wedge\varphi_{f}\wedge y.\iota.f=t[x/\mathsf{heap}]\}\rrbracket_{[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}\subseteq

\displaystyle\llbracket\{y:\top\mid\varphi_{pkt}\wedge\varphi_{\iota}\wedge\varphi_{f}\wedge y.\iota.f=t[x/\mathsf{heap}]\}\rrbracket_{[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}

The result is immediate.

Case

T-Remit
::

By inversion of rule
T-Remit
, we get

(A1):

\mathsf{Includes}~\Gamma~\tau~\iota

(A2):

\tau_{2}=\Sigma y\!:\{z:\tau_{1}\mid z\equiv x\}.\{z:\epsilon\mid z.{pkt_{in}}=\langle\rangle\wedge z.pkt_{out}=x.\iota\}

Let
\tau_{2}^{\prime}=\Sigma y\!:\{z:\tau_{1}^{\prime}\mid z\equiv x\}.\{z:\epsilon\mid z.{pkt_{in}}=\langle\rangle\wedge z.pkt_{out}=x.\iota\}
.

\mathsf{Includes}_{\iota}~\Gamma^{\prime}~\tau_{1}^{\prime}
follows by assumptions
(A1)
and
(A)
and set theory.

(1)
follows by
T-Remit
.
Let
h\in\llbracket\tau_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
be arbitrary.

\displaystyle\llbracket\Sigma y\!:\{z:\tau_{1}\mid z\equiv x\}.\{z:\epsilon\mid z.{pkt_{in}}=\langle\rangle\wedge z.pkt_{out}=x.\iota\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{h_{1}+\!\!+\ h_{2}\mid h_{1}\in\llbracket\{z:\tau_{1}\mid z\equiv x\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h]}\wedge

\displaystyle\hskip 45.5pth_{2}\in\llbracket\{z:\epsilon\mid z.{pkt_{in}}=\langle\rangle\wedge z.pkt_{out}=x.\iota\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h,y\mapsto h_{1}]}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{h+\!\!+\ h_{2}\mid h_{2}\in\llbracket\{z:\epsilon\mid z.{pkt_{in}}=\langle\rangle\wedge z.pkt_{out}=x.\iota\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h,y\mapsto h]}\}

x
is the only free variable in
\{z:\epsilon\mid z.{pkt_{in}}=\langle\rangle\wedge z.pkt_{out}=x.\iota\}
, which maps to the same heap
h
in both environments
\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h,y\mapsto h]
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h,y\mapsto h]
.

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{h+\!\!+\ h_{2}\mid h_{2}\in\llbracket\{z:\epsilon\mid z.{pkt_{in}}=\langle\rangle\wedge z.pkt_{out}=x.\iota\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h,y\mapsto h]}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{h_{1}+\!\!+\ h_{2}\mid h_{1}\in\llbracket\{z:\tau_{1}^{\prime}\mid z\equiv x\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h]}\wedge

\displaystyle\hskip 45.5pth_{2}\in\llbracket\{z:\epsilon\mid z.{pkt_{in}}=\langle\rangle\wedge z.pkt_{out}=x.\iota\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h,y\mapsto h_{1}]}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\llbracket\Sigma y\!:\{z:\tau_{1}^{\prime}\mid z\equiv x\}.\{z:\epsilon\mid z.{pkt_{in}}=\langle\rangle\wedge z.pkt_{out}=x.\iota\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h]}

This concludes the case by showing
(2)
.

Case

::

By inversion of rule
T-Reset
, we get

(A1):

c=\mathit{reset}

(A2):

\tau_{2}=\Sigma y\!:\{z:\epsilon\mid z.pkt_{out}=\langle\rangle\wedge z.{pkt_{in}}=x.pkt_{out}\}.\{z:\epsilon\mid z.pkt_{out}=\langle\rangle\wedge z.{pkt_{in}}=x.{pkt_{in}}\}

Let
\tau_{2}^{\prime}=\tau_{2}
.
(1)
follows by
T-Reset
.
Let
h_{1}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\tau_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
be arbitrary.

\displaystyle\llbracket\Sigma y\!:\{z:\epsilon\mid z.pkt_{out}=\langle\rangle\wedge z.{pkt_{in}}=x.pkt_{out}\}.

\displaystyle\hskip 22.5pt\{z:\epsilon\mid z.pkt_{out}=\langle\rangle\wedge z.{pkt_{in}}=x.{pkt_{in}}\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}\subseteq

\displaystyle\llbracket\Sigma y\!:\{z:\epsilon\mid z.pkt_{out}=\langle\rangle\wedge z.{pkt_{in}}=x.pkt_{out}\}.

\displaystyle\hskip 22.5pt\{z:\epsilon\mid z.pkt_{out}=\langle\rangle\wedge z.{pkt_{in}}=x.{pkt_{in}}\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}

Both sets are actually equal, because
x
is the only free variable in
\tau_{2}
and
\tau_{2}^{\prime}
respectively.
Thus, all other bindings in the environments
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
have no effect on the semantics of
\tau_{2}
and
\tau_{2}^{\prime}
respectively.
This shows
(2)
and concludes the case.

Case

T-Seq
::

By inversion of rule
T-Seq
, we get

(A1):

c=c_{1};c_{2}

(A2):

\Gamma\vdash c_{1}:(x:\tau_{1})\to\tau_{12}

(A3):

\Gamma,x:\tau_{1}\vdash c_{2}:(y:\tau_{12})\to\tau_{22}

(A4):

\tau_{2}=\tau_{22}[y\mapsto\tau_{12}]

By IH with
(A2)
,
(A)
,
(B)
and
(C)
,
there exists some
\tau_{12}^{\prime}
such that

(A5):

\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash c_{1}:(x:\tau_{1}^{\prime})\to\tau_{12}^{\prime}

(A6):

\forall h_{1}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\tau_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}.\llbracket\tau_{12}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}\subseteq\llbracket\tau_{12}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}

Apply the IH again to
c_{2}
:

Let
h_{1}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\tau_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
be arbitrary.
By
(A3)

\Gamma,x:\tau_{1}\vdash c_{2}:(y:\tau_{12})\to\tau_{22}
.
By
(A6)
,
\llbracket\tau_{12}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}\subseteq\llbracket\tau_{12}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}
.

\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]\models\Gamma,x:\tau_{1}
because by assumption
\mathcal{E}\models\Gamma
the entailment holds for all
x_{i}\neq x
.
For
x
there exists a binding to heap
h_{1}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}
(with assumption
(A)
) and the entailment between
h
and
\tau_{1}
trivially holds.

To show
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]\models\Gamma^{\prime},x:\tau_{1}^{\prime}
, we must show that

\forall x_{i},\tau_{i}.\Gamma^{\prime}(x_{i})=\tau_{i}\Rightarrow\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}](x_{i})=h_{i}\wedge h_{i}\models_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}\tau_{i}
.
Case
x_{i}\neq x
: this holds by assumption
(C)
.
Case
x_{i}=x
.
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}](x)=h_{1}^{\prime}
.
To show that
h_{1}^{\prime}\models_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}\tau_{1}^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow h_{1}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\tau_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}
.
By assumption,
x
is not free in
\tau_{1}^{\prime}
, so we can equivalently show that
h_{1}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\tau_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
, which holds by assumption.

Again by IH, there exists some
\tau_{22}^{\prime}
such that

(A7):

\Gamma^{\prime},x:\tau_{1}^{\prime}\vdash c_{2}(y:\tau_{12}^{\prime})\to\tau_{22}^{\prime}

(A8):

\forall h_{12}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\tau_{12}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}.\llbracket\tau_{22}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h_{1},y\mapsto h_{12}^{\prime}]}\subseteq\llbracket\tau_{22}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h_{1},y\mapsto h_{12}^{\prime}]}

Let
\tau_{2}^{\prime}=\tau_{22}^{\prime}[y\mapsto\tau_{12}^{\prime}]
.
(1)
follows by
T-Seq
.

For
(2)
, we must show that
\forall h_{1}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\tau_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}.\llbracket\tau_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}\subseteq\llbracket\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}

Let
h_{1}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\tau_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
be arbitrary.

\displaystyle\llbracket\tau_{22}^{\prime}[y\mapsto\tau_{12}^{\prime}]\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}\subseteq\llbracket\tau_{22}[y\mapsto\tau_{12}]\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\bigcup_{h_{12}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\tau_{12}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}}\llbracket\tau_{22}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime},y\mapsto h_{12}^{\prime}]}\subseteq\bigcup_{h_{12}\in\llbracket\tau_{12}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}}\llbracket\tau_{22}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime},y\mapsto h_{12}]}

The result follows by
(A6)
,
(A8)
and set theory.

Case

T-Skip
::

By inversion of rule
T-Skip
, we get

(A1):

c=\mathit{skip}

(A2):

\tau_{2}=\{y:\tau_{1}\mid y\equiv x\}

\tau_{2}^{\prime}=\{y:\tau_{1}^{\prime}\mid y\equiv x\}
.

follows by

.

To show

\displaystyle\llbracket\{y:\tau_{1}^{\prime}\mid y\equiv x\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h^{\prime}]}\subseteq\llbracket\{y:\tau_{1}\mid y\equiv x\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\{h^{\prime}\}\subseteq\{h^{\prime}\}

by
(A)

Case

T-Sub
::

By inversion of rule
T-Sub
, we get

(A1):

\Gamma\vdash c:(x:\tau_{3})\to\tau_{4}

(A2):

\Gamma\vdash\tau_{1}<:\tau_{3}

\Gamma,x\!:\!\tau_{1}\vdash\tau_{4}<:\tau_{2}

By assumption

\llbracket\tau_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}\subseteq\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}

and from
(A2)

\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}\subseteq\llbracket\tau_{3}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}

and thus

.

\tau_{4}^{\prime}

\forall h^{\prime}\in\llbracket\tau_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}.\llbracket\tau_{4}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[x\mapsto h^{\prime}]}\subseteq\llbracket\tau_{4}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\tau_{2}^{\prime}=\tau_{4}^{\prime}
.

(1)
follows by

.

For

, we have to show that

, which follows by
(A3)
and
(A5)
and by set theory.

∎

Lemma B.12 (Semantic Chomp Expression).

For all expressions
e
,
heaps
h
and
h^{\prime}
,
environments
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
and variables
x
,
if
h^{\prime}=\mathsf{chomp}^{\Downarrow}(h,1)
, and

\mathcal{E}^{\prime}=\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto(\langle\rangle,\langle\rangle,[\iota\mapsto v])]
and,
if
x\in\mathit{dom}(\mathcal{E})
,
v=\mathcal{E}(x)(\iota)@h({pkt_{in}})[0\!:\!1]
and

\mathcal{E}(x)({pkt_{in}})=\langle\rangle
and
\mathcal{E}(x)(pkt_{out})=\langle\rangle
,
and otherwise
v=h({pkt_{in}})[0\!:\!1]
and
x
not free in
e
,
then

\llbracket e\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}=\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(e,y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

Proof.

Proof by induction on
e
.
We only consider expressions referencing
{pkt_{in}}
.
All other expressions are not affected by chomping, and therefore the semantic is unchanged.

Case

e=z.{pkt_{in}}[l\!:\!r]
::

Case distinction on
z=y
:

Subcase

z\neq y
::

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(z.{pkt_{in}}[l\!:\!r],y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(z.{pkt_{in}}[l\!:\!r],\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket z.{pkt_{in}}[l\!:\!r]\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

If
z\neq x
,
z
binds to some heap in
\mathcal{E}
, which must also be contained in
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
unchanged.
If
z=x
, by assumption,
x.{pkt_{in}}
maps to the empty bit vector, both in
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
.

\displaystyle=\llbracket z.{pkt_{in}}[l\!:\!r]\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

Subcase

z=y,r\leq 1
::

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(y.{pkt_{in}}[0\!:\!1],y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(y.{pkt_{in}}[0\!:\!1],y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{b_{0}}::\langle\rangle,\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket x.\iota[\mathsf{sizeof}(\iota)-1\!:\!\mathsf{sizeof}(\iota)-1+1]@\langle\rangle\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket x.\iota[\lvert v\rvert-1\!:\!\lvert v\rvert]\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(x)(\iota)[\lvert v\rvert-1]

\displaystyle=h({pkt_{in}})[0\!:\!1]

\displaystyle=\llbracket y.{pkt_{in}}[0\!:\!1]\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

Subcase

z=y,l=0
::

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(y.{pkt_{in}}[0\!:\!r],y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(y.{pkt_{in}}[0\!:\!r],y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{b_{0}}::y.{pkt_{in}}[0\!:\!r-1],\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket x.\iota[\mathsf{sizeof}(\iota)-1\!:\!\mathsf{sizeof}(\iota)-1+1]@y.{pkt_{in}}[0\!:\!r-1]\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket x.\iota[\lvert v\rvert-1\!:\!\lvert v\rvert]@y.{pkt_{in}}[0\!:\!r-1]\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(x)(\iota)[\lvert v\rvert-1\!:\!\lvert v\rvert]@h^{\prime}({pkt_{in}})[0\!:\!r-1]

with
v=\mathcal{E}(x)(\iota)@h({pkt_{in}})[0]
follows

\displaystyle=h({pkt_{in}})[0\!:\!1]@h^{\prime}({pkt_{in}})[0\!:\!r-1]

with
h^{\prime}=\mathsf{chomp}^{\Downarrow}(h,1)
follows

\displaystyle=h({pkt_{in}})[0\!:\!1]@h({pkt_{in}})[1\!:\!r]

\displaystyle=h({pkt_{in}})[0\!:\!r]

\displaystyle=\llbracket y.{pkt_{in}}[0\!:\!r]\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

Subcase

z=y,l\neq 0
::

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(y.{pkt_{in}}[l\!:\!r],y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(y.{pkt_{in}}[l\!:\!r],y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(y.{pkt_{in}}[l-1\!:\!r-1],\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket y.{pkt_{in}}[l-1\!:\!r-1]\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=h^{\prime}({pkt_{in}})[l-1\!:\!r-1]

with
h^{\prime}=\mathsf{chomp}^{\Downarrow}(h,1)
follows

\displaystyle=h({pkt_{in}})[l\!:\!r]

\displaystyle=\llbracket y.{pkt_{in}}[l\!:\!r]\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

Case

e=z.{pkt_{in}}
::

Case distinction on
z=y
:

Subcase

z\neq y
::

Symmetric to first subcase of previous case.

Subcase

z=y
::

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(y.{pkt_{in}},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{b_{0}}::y.{pkt_{in}},\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket x.\iota[\mathsf{sizeof}(\iota)-1\!:\!\mathsf{sizeof}(\iota)-1+1]@y.{pkt_{in}}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket x.\iota[\lvert v\rvert-1\!:\!\lvert v\rvert]@y.{pkt_{in}}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=h({pkt_{in}})[0\!:\!1]@h^{\prime}({pkt_{in}})

with
h^{\prime}=\mathsf{chomp}^{\Downarrow}(h,1)
follows

\displaystyle=h({pkt_{in}})

\displaystyle=\llbracket y.{pkt_{in}}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

Case

e=\lvert z.{pkt_{in}}\rvert
::

Case distinction on
z=y
:

Subcase

z\neq y
::

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(\lvert z.{pkt_{in}}\rvert,y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\lvert z.{pkt_{in}}\rvert\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

If
z=x
, the length of
x.{pkt_{in}}=0
in both environments and otherwise,
z.{pkt_{in}}
refers to the same heap in both
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
.

\displaystyle=\llbracket\lvert z.{pkt_{in}}\rvert\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

Subcase

z=y
::

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(\lvert y.{pkt_{in}}\rvert,y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\lvert y.{pkt_{in}}\rvert+1\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

with
h^{\prime}=\mathsf{chomp}^{\Downarrow}(h,1)
follows

\displaystyle=\llbracket\lvert y.{pkt_{in}}\rvert\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

Case

e=b::bv
::

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(b::bv,y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(b::\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(bv,y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket b::\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(bv,y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket b\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}::\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(bv,y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket b\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}::\llbracket bv\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

by IH follows

\displaystyle=\llbracket b\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}::\llbracket bv\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

since
b
is either
0
or
1

\displaystyle=\llbracket b::bv\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

Case

e=bv_{1}@bv_{2}
::

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(bv_{1}@bv_{2},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(bv_{1},y,\mathsf{b_{0}})@\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(bv_{2},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(bv_{1},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}@

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{e}_{1}(bv_{1},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket bv_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}@\llbracket bv_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

by IH

\displaystyle=\llbracket bv_{1}@bv_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

Case

e=n+m
::

Symmetric to previous case.

∎

Lemma B.13 (Semantic Chomp Formulae).

For all formulae
\varphi
, heaps
h
and
h^{\prime}
,
environments
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
and variables
x
,
if
h^{\prime}=\mathsf{chomp}^{\Downarrow}(h,1)
, and

\mathcal{E}^{\prime}=\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto(\langle\rangle,\langle\rangle,[\iota\mapsto v])]
,
and if
x\in\mathit{dom}
,
v=\mathcal{E}(x)(\iota)@h({pkt_{in}})[0\!:\!1]

and
\mathcal{E}(x)({pkt_{in}})=\langle\rangle
and
\mathcal{E}(x)(pkt_{out})=\langle\rangle
and
otherwise
v=h({pkt_{in}})[0\!:\!1]
and
x
not free in
\varphi
, then

\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}=\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(\varphi,y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

Proof.

By induction on
\varphi
.

Case

\varphi=e_{1}=e_{2}
::

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(e_{1}=e_{2},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(e_{1},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota)\ =

\displaystyle\hskip 14.5pt\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(e_{1},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(e_{1},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}=

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(e_{1},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

by
Lemma

B.12
follows

\displaystyle=\llbracket e_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}=\llbracket e_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket e_{1}=e_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

Case

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}
::

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(\varphi_{1},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\land

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}\llbracket}\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(\varphi_{2},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(\varphi_{1},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}\land

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(\varphi_{2},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

by IH follows

\displaystyle=\llbracket\varphi_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}\land\llbracket\varphi_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

Case

\varphi=\neg\varphi_{1}
::

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(\neg\varphi_{1},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\neg\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(\varphi_{1},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h]}

\displaystyle=\neg\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(\varphi_{1},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h]}

by IH follows

\displaystyle=\neg\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\neg\varphi\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

Case

\varphi=z.\iota^{\prime}.valid
::

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(z.\iota^{\prime}.valid,y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket z.\iota^{\prime}.valid\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\iota^{\prime}\in dom(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}](z))

\displaystyle=\iota^{\prime}\in dom(\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h](z))

by definition of
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
and
h^{\prime}
follows

\displaystyle=\llbracket z.\iota^{\prime}.valid\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

Case

\varphi=\mathsf{true}
::

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(\mathsf{true},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathsf{true}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h]}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{true}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathsf{true}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

Case

\varphi=\mathsf{false}
::

Symmetric to previous case.

∎

Lemma B.14 (Semantic Chomp Refinement).

For all heap types
\tau
, heaps
h
and
h^{\prime}
, environments
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
and variables
x
,
if
h^{\prime}=\mathsf{chomp}^{\Downarrow}(h,1)
, and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}=\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto(\langle\rangle,\langle\rangle,[\iota\mapsto v])]
, and,
if
x\in\mathit{dom}
,
v=\mathcal{E}(x)(\iota)@h({pkt_{in}})[0\!:\!1]
and
\mathcal{E}(x)({pkt_{in}})=\langle\rangle
and
\mathcal{E}(x)(pkt_{out})=\langle\rangle
,
and otherwise
v=h({pkt_{in}})[0\!:\!1]
and
x
not free in
\tau
, then

\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}=\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau,y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

Proof.

Proof by induction on
\tau
.

Case

\tau=\varnothing
::

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\varnothing,y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\varnothing\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\{\}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

Case

\tau=\top
::

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\top,y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\top\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=H

\displaystyle=\llbracket\top\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}}

Case

\tau=\Sigma z\!:\tau_{1}.\tau_{2}
::

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\Sigma z\!:\tau_{1}.\tau_{2},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\Sigma z\!:\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau_{1},x,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1).

\displaystyle\phantom{{}=\llbracket\Sigma z:\!!}\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau_{2},x,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\{h_{1}^{\prime}+\!\!+\ h_{2}^{\prime}\mid h_{1}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau_{1},x,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}~\land

\displaystyle\hskip 58.0pth_{2}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau_{2},x,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime},z\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}\}

\displaystyle=\{h_{1}^{\prime}+\!\!+\ h_{2}^{\prime}\mid h_{1}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}\land h_{2}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h,z\mapsto h_{1}^{\prime}]}\}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\Sigma z\!:\tau_{1}.\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

Case

\tau=\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}
::

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau_{1}+\tau_{2},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau_{1},x,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)~+

\displaystyle\hskip 15.00002pt\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau_{2},x,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau_{1},x,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}~\cup

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau_{2},x,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}\cup\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

Case

\tau=\{z:\tau_{1}\mid\varphi\}
::

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\{z:\tau_{1}\mid\varphi\},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\{z:\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau_{1},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\mid

\displaystyle\hskip 34.0pt\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(\varphi,y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\{h\mid h\in\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau_{1},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}~\land

\displaystyle\hskip 46.0pt\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chomp}^{\varphi}_{1}(\varphi,y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}=\mathsf{true}\}

\displaystyle=\{h\mid h\in\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}\land\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}=\mathsf{true}\}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\{z:\tau_{1}\mid\varphi\}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

Case

\tau=\tau_{1}{[z\mapsto\tau_{2}]}
::

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau_{1}[z\mapsto\tau_{2}],y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau_{1},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)[z\mapsto

\displaystyle\hskip 40.0pt\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau_{2},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)]\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\{h\mid h_{2}\in\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau_{2},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime}]}\land

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pth\in\llbracket\mathsf{heapRef}_{1}(\mathsf{chompRef}_{1}(\tau_{1},y,\mathsf{b_{0}}),\mathsf{b_{0}},x,\iota,1\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[y\mapsto h^{\prime},z\mapsto h_{2}]}\}

\displaystyle=\{h\mid h_{2}\in\llbracket\tau_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}\land h\in\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h,z\mapsto h_{2}]}\}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\tau_{1}[z\mapsto\tau_{2}]\rrbracket_{\mathcal{E}[y\mapsto h]}

∎

Lemma B.15 (Semantic Chomp
1
).

For all heap types
\tau
, environments
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
, and variables
x
,
if
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}=\mathcal{E}[x\mapsto(\langle\rangle,\langle\rangle,[\iota\mapsto v])]
, and
if
x\in\mathit{dom}(\mathcal{E})
,
v=\mathcal{E}(x)(\iota)@h({pkt_{in}})[0\!:\!1]
and
\mathcal{E}(x)({pkt_{in}})=\mathcal{E}(x)(pkt_{out})=\langle\rangle
, otherwise
v=h({pkt_{in}})[0\!:\!1]
, then
